- **(C) Gastrointestinal stromal tumor (GIST):** This is incorrect. GIST is a mesenchymal tumor, not a lymphoma. It arises from interstitial cells of Cajal and is characterized by CD117 (c-KIT) positivity. It is not associated with celiac disease or aberrant IEL proliferation.
- **(D) Pancreatic neuroendocrine tumor overproducing VIP:** This is incorrect. A VIPoma causes severe secretory diarrhea (WDHA syndrome) due to excessive VIP secretion. While it causes diarrhea, it does not explain the jejunal ulcers, strictures, fever, night sweats, or the specific IEL findings.
- **(E) Squamous cell carcinoma of the anal canal:** This is incorrect. Anal SCC is a malignancy of the anal canal, presenting with perianal symptoms. It is unrelated to celiac disease or the findings in the proximal jejunum.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Celiac Disease (CD) | Refractory Celiac Disease (RCD) | EATL (Complication of RCD Type 2) | Other Options (B, C, D, E) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------ | :------------------------------ | :-------------------------------- | :-------------------------- |
| **Underlying Condition** | Autoimmune          | Complication of CD              | Malignancy arising from RCD Type 2 | Unrelated                  |
| **GFD Adherence**       | Improves symptoms   | Symptoms persist despite GFD     | Symptoms persist despite GFD     | N/A                        |
| **Symptoms**            | Malabsorption       | Severe diarrhea, weight loss, fever | Severe diarrhea, weight loss, fever, B symptoms | Variable                   |
| **Endoscopy**           | Villous atrophy     | Ulcers, strictures               | Ulcers, strictures               | Variable                   |
| **Biopsy**              | Increased IELs      | Increased, atypical IELs         | Clonal, aberrant IELs (CD3+, CD8-, CD4-, CD5-, clonal TCR-gamma) | Variable                   |
| **Immunophenotype**     | Normal IELs         | Polyclonal IELs (Type 1 RCD)     | Clonal, aberrant IELs            | Variable                   |
| **Risk**                | Low                 | High risk of EATL (Type 2)       | Malignancy                      | N/A                        |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Long-standing celiac disease, particularly when refractory to a strict gluten-free diet (Type 2 RCD), carries a significant risk of developing Enteropathy-Associated T-Cell Lymphoma (EATL). Recognizing the clinical presentation (refractory symptoms, B symptoms) and characteristic biopsy findings (clonal, aberrant intraepithelial lymphocytes with a specific immunophenotype) is crucial for diagnosis and management. This highlights the importance of monitoring patients with RCD Type 2 closely for signs of malignancy.

---

### Question 125: Malabsorption Syndromes: Tropical Sprue (Travel/Residence in Tropics), Megaloblastic Anemia, and Tetracycline
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating Tropical Sprue from Celiac Disease based on pathophysiology, affected intestinal segments, nutritional deficiencies, etiology, treatment response, and histological findings. (区分热带营养不良症与乳糜泻的病理生理学、受累小肠段、营养缺乏、病因、治疗反应和组织学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 44-year-old humanitarian worker returns to the United States after living in Puerto Rico for 2 years. Over the past 4 months, he has developed chronic watery diarrhea, severe fatigue, abdominal cramps, and an 8-kg weight loss. Laboratory studies demonstrate: macrocytic megaloblastic anemia (Hb 8.2 g/dL, MCV 112 fL) with depressed serum Folate (1.8 ng/mL) and low Vitamin B12 (90 pg/mL). Celiac serologies are negative. Upper endoscopy with duodenal and jejunal biopsies demonstrates partial villous blunting and lymphocytic infiltration involving both the jejunum and distal ileum. Which of the following features fundamentally differentiates Tropical Sprue from Celiac Disease?

(A) Tropical sprue affects the entire small intestine (especially the Jejunum and Ileum, causing combined Folate and Vitamin B12 deficiency), is presumed to be triggered by chronic coliform bacterial overgrowth/toxins in tropical regions, does not respond to a gluten-free diet, and achieves complete clinical cure with a prolonged course of Oral Tetracycline (or Doxycycline) plus Folic Acid
(B) Tropical sprue affects exclusively the stomach and is cured with PPIs
(C) Tropical sprue is an autoimmune disorder linked to HLA-DQ2
(D) Tropical sprue never causes diarrhea and presents with severe constipation
(E) Tropical sprue is caused by infection with the nematode Strongyloides

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 44-year-old male, humanitarian worker, recent return from Puerto Rico (tropical region) after 2 years. This history strongly suggests exposure to conditions prevalent in tropical areas.
- **Symptoms:** Chronic watery diarrhea, fatigue, abdominal cramps, weight loss (8 kg over 4 months). These are classic symptoms of malabsorption syndromes.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Macrocytic megaloblastic anemia (Hb 8.2 g/dL, MCV 112 fL) indicates impaired DNA synthesis, often due to folate or vitamin B12 deficiency. Low serum Folate (1.8 ng/mL, normal ~3-17 ng/mL) and low Vitamin B12 (90 pg/mL, normal ~200-900 pg/mL) confirm deficiencies of both. This combined deficiency is a key feature.
- **Negative Celiac Serologies:** Rules out Celiac Disease as the primary diagnosis, although overlap can occur.
- **Endoscopy/Biopsy:** Partial villous blunting and lymphocytic infiltration in both jejunum and distal ileum. This indicates small intestinal mucosal damage affecting multiple segments, consistent with malabsorption.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The combination of tropical exposure, chronic diarrhea, weight loss, combined folate and B12 deficiency, and small intestinal mucosal changes points towards Tropical Sprue. The question asks for the differentiating feature from Celiac Disease.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (消化道分泌生理与上皮转运机制推导)
- **Celiac Disease:** Autoimmune reaction to gluten in genetically susceptible individuals (HLA-DQ2/DQ8). Primarily affects the proximal small intestine (duodenum and jejunum), causing villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, and intraepithelial lymphocytosis. Leads mainly to fat malabsorption (steatorrhea) and deficiencies of fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K), iron, folate, and calcium. Vitamin B12 deficiency is less common unless the ileum is also involved (e.g., Crohn's disease). Responds to a strict gluten-free diet.
- **Tropical Sprue:** An acquired malabsorption syndrome occurring in tropical regions. Pathophysiology is not fully understood but is thought to involve chronic infection with coliform bacteria or their toxins, leading to small intestinal mucosal damage. Affects the jejunum and ileum, often more severely than the duodenum. Characterized by villous blunting, crypt hyperplasia, and inflammatory cell infiltration. Causes malabsorption of multiple nutrients, including fats, carbohydrates, proteins, vitamins (especially folate and B12), and minerals. The combined folate and B12 deficiency is a hallmark, likely due to widespread jejunal and ileal involvement. Unlike Celiac disease, it does not respond to a gluten-free diet. Treatment involves long-term antibiotics (tetracycline or doxycycline) to reduce bacterial overgrowth and folic acid supplementation.
- **Comparison:**
    - **Affected Area:** Celiac primarily proximal (duodenum/jejunum); Tropical Sprue affects jejunum and ileum.
    - **Deficiencies:** Celiac primarily fat-soluble vitamins, iron, folate; Tropical Sprue combined folate and B12 deficiency is characteristic.
    - **Etiology:** Celiac is autoimmune (gluten); Tropical Sprue is presumed infectious/bacterial toxin-related.
    - **Treatment:** Celiac responds to gluten-free diet; Tropical Sprue requires antibiotics (tetracycline) and folate.
    - **Pathology:** Both cause villous blunting and inflammation, but the distribution and specific triggers differ.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify the likely diagnosis:** The patient's history (tropical residence), symptoms (chronic diarrhea, weight loss), lab findings (macrocytic anemia, low folate, low B12), negative celiac serology, and biopsy results (jejunal/ileal villous blunting) strongly suggest Tropical Sprue.
2.  **Recall the key features of Tropical Sprue:** Affects jejunum and ileum, causes combined folate and B12 deficiency, is associated with tropical regions, does not respond to a gluten-free diet, and is treated with tetracycline and folate.
3.  **Recall the key features of Celiac Disease:** Primarily affects duodenum/jejunum, causes mainly fat malabsorption and folate deficiency (B12 less common), is autoimmune, triggered by gluten, and treated with a gluten-free diet.
4.  **Evaluate the options based on the differentiation:**
    - (A) Accurately describes features differentiating Tropical Sprue (jejunal/ileal involvement, combined B12/folate deficiency, bacterial trigger, no gluten-free diet response, tetracycline treatment) from Celiac Disease.
    - (B) Incorrect. Tropical Sprue affects the small intestine, not exclusively the stomach.
    - (C) Incorrect. Tropical Sprue is not primarily autoimmune; Celiac Disease is.
    - (D) Incorrect. Tropical Sprue characteristically causes diarrhea.
    - (E) Incorrect. Tropical Sprue is not caused by Strongyloides; Strongyloides can cause malabsorption but has a different etiology and presentation.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Tropical sprue affects the entire small intestine (especially the Jejunum and Ileum, causing combined Folate and Vitamin B12 deficiency), is presumed to be triggered by chronic coliform bacterial overgrowth/toxins in tropical regions, does not respond to a gluten-free diet, and achieves complete clinical cure with a prolonged course of Oral Tetracycline (or Doxycycline) plus Folic Acid:** This option correctly highlights the key differentiating features of Tropical Sprue compared to Celiac Disease: the involvement of both jejunum and ileum leading to combined B12 and folate deficiency, the presumed infectious etiology, the lack of response to a gluten-free diet, and the specific treatment with tetracycline and folic acid. This aligns perfectly with the clinical presentation and known pathophysiology.
- **(B) Tropical sprue affects exclusively the stomach and is cured with PPIs:** This is incorrect. Tropical Sprue affects the small intestine (jejunum and ileum), not the stomach. Treatment involves antibiotics and folate, not PPIs.
- **(C) Tropical sprue is an autoimmune disorder linked to HLA-DQ2:** This describes Celiac Disease, not Tropical Sprue. Tropical Sprue is thought to be related to chronic bacterial infection or toxin exposure.
- **(D) Tropical sprue never causes diarrhea and presents with severe constipation:** This is incorrect. Chronic watery diarrhea is a hallmark symptom of Tropical Sprue.
- **(E) Tropical sprue is caused by infection with the nematode Strongyloides:** This is incorrect. While Strongyloides infection can cause gastrointestinal symptoms and malabsorption, Tropical Sprue is a distinct entity, likely related to bacterial overgrowth or toxins.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Celiac Disease                                  | Tropical Sprue                                     |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- |
| **Etiology**            | Autoimmune (Gluten)                             | Presumed infectious (Bacterial overgrowth/toxins)   |
| **Genetics**            | HLA-DQ2/DQ8                                     | None identified                                    |
| **Affected Area**       | Primarily Duodenum/Jejunum                      | Jejunum and Ileum                                  |
| **Key Deficiencies**    | Folate, Iron, Fat-soluble vitamins, Ca           | Folate, Vitamin B12                                |
| **Dietary Trigger**     | Gluten                                          | None identified                                    |
| **Treatment**           | Gluten-free diet                                | Tetracycline/Doxycycline + Folic Acid               |
| **Response to Gluten-Free Diet** | Yes                                           | No                                                 |
| **Typical Presentation** | Steatorrhea, weight loss, anemia, bloating      | Chronic diarrhea, weight loss, anemia (megaloblastic) |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Tropical Sprue is a malabsorption syndrome acquired in tropical regions, distinct from Celiac Disease. Key differentiators include the involvement of both jejunum and ileum leading to combined folate and B12 deficiency, a presumed infectious etiology (bacterial overgrowth/toxins), lack of response to a gluten-free diet, and treatment with long-term antibiotics (tetracycline) plus folic acid supplementation. Celiac disease is an autoimmune disorder triggered by gluten, primarily affecting the proximal small intestine and responding to a gluten-free diet.

---

### Question 126: Infectious Malabsorption: Whipple Disease (Tropheryma whipplei), PAS-Positive Diastase-Resistant Macrophages
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the recognition of Whipple disease based on its systemic manifestations (arthralgias, diarrhea, weight loss, neurological symptoms, hyperpigmentation), endoscopic findings (duodenal plaques), and characteristic histopathology (PAS-positive, diastase-resistant macrophages in lamina propria), linking it to its causative agent, *Tropheryma whipplei*.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old white male farmer presents with a 2-year history of migratory, non-deforming polyarthralgias involving his knees and ankles, followed over the past 4 months by chronic diarrhea, abdominal distension, severe weight loss (14 kg), hyperpigmentation of sun-exposed skin, generalized lymphadenopathy, and low-grade fevers. Neurological exam reveals subtle cognitive impairment and supranuclear ophthalmoplegia. Upper endoscopy reveals pale yellow-white shaggy mucosal plaques in the postbulbar duodenum. Small bowel biopsy demonstrates diffuse widening and expansion of the lamina propria by foamy, plump macrophages packed with intracellular granular material that stains intensely positive with Periodic Acid-Schiff (PAS) and is resistant to diastase digestion; acid-fast staining is negative. What is the diagnosis and its causative pathogen?

(A) Whipple disease, caused by *Tropheryma whipplei* (an actinomycete gram-positive bacillus); systemic multi-organ infection characterized by the classic tetrad of Arthralgias, Diarrhea/Malabsorption, Abdominal pain, and Weight loss, often with neurological and cardiac involvement (endocarditis); treated with intravenous ceftriaxone followed by 1 year of oral trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole
(B) *Mycobacterium avium complex* (MAC) infection in severe AIDS
(C) Celiac disease with Marsh type 3 villous atrophy
(D) Intestinal amyloidosis AL with Apple-green birefringence
(E) Giardiasis with pear-shaped flagellated trophozoites

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old white male farmer. Age and demographics are relevant as Whipple disease typically affects middle-aged white men.
- **Symptoms:** 2-year history of migratory polyarthralgias (knees, ankles), 4-month history of chronic diarrhea, abdominal distension, severe weight loss (14 kg), hyperpigmentation, generalized lymphadenopathy, low-grade fevers. This constellation of symptoms points towards a systemic inflammatory or infectious process affecting multiple organ systems, including joints, GI tract (malabsorption), skin, lymph nodes, and potentially the CNS.
- **Neurological Findings:** Subtle cognitive impairment and supranuclear ophthalmoplegia. These are characteristic neurological manifestations of Whipple disease, indicating CNS involvement.
- **Endoscopy:** Pale yellow-white shaggy mucosal plaques in the postbulbar duodenum. This finding is highly suggestive of Whipple disease, although not pathognomonic.
- **Biopsy:** Diffuse widening of lamina propria by foamy macrophages containing PAS-positive, diastase-resistant granular material. This is the hallmark histopathological finding of Whipple disease. The macrophages are filled with the causative organism. Acid-fast staining is negative, ruling out mycobacterial infections.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (消化道分泌生理与上皮转运机制推导)
- **Whipple Disease Pathophysiology:** Whipple disease is a rare, systemic infectious disease caused by the bacterium *Tropheryma whipplei*. The organism primarily affects the small intestine, leading to malabsorption, but can disseminate to virtually any organ system, including joints, brain, heart, eyes, and lymph nodes.
- **Infection Mechanism:** *T. whipplei* is an intracellular bacterium that invades macrophages, particularly in the lamina propria of the small intestine. The bacteria replicate within these macrophages, causing them to become enlarged and foamy.
- **Histopathology:** The characteristic finding is the accumulation of these PAS-positive, diastase-resistant macrophages in the lamina propria. The PAS stain highlights the bacterial cell walls, which contain complex carbohydrates. Diastase resistance indicates that the material is not glycogen. Acid-fast staining is negative because *T. whipplei* lacks mycolic acids.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The diverse clinical presentation reflects the systemic nature of the infection. GI symptoms (diarrhea, steatorrhea, abdominal pain, weight loss) result from malabsorption due to villous atrophy and impaired nutrient transport. Arthralgias are common. Neurological symptoms (cognitive impairment, ophthalmoplegia, seizures, dementia) occur in a significant proportion of patients due to CNS infiltration. Hyperpigmentation, fever, lymphadenopathy, and endocarditis can also occur.
- **Diagnosis:** Diagnosis relies on identifying the characteristic macrophages in biopsy specimens (typically from the duodenum or jejunum) and confirming the presence of *T. whipplei* using PCR or immunohistochemistry.
- **Treatment:** Prompt treatment with antibiotics (typically intravenous ceftriaxone followed by long-term oral trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) is crucial to prevent irreversible organ damage.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify Key Symptoms:** The patient presents with a classic combination of migratory arthralgias, chronic diarrhea, weight loss, neurological symptoms (cognitive impairment, ophthalmoplegia), and skin hyperpigmentation. This suggests a systemic disease.
2.  **Evaluate Endoscopic Findings:** The presence of pale yellow-white shaggy plaques in the duodenum points towards an inflammatory or infectious process affecting the small intestinal mucosa.
3.  **Analyze Biopsy Results:** The definitive finding is the diffuse expansion of the lamina propria by PAS-positive, diastase-resistant macrophages. This is pathognomonic for Whipple disease.
4.  **Consider Differential Diagnoses:** Based on the clinical picture and biopsy findings, evaluate the options provided.
5.  **Confirm Diagnosis:** Option A accurately describes Whipple disease, its causative agent (*Tropheryma whipplei*), its characteristic clinical features (tetrad of arthralgias, diarrhea/malabsorption, abdominal pain, weight loss, plus neurological/cardiac involvement), and the hallmark histopathology (PAS-positive macrophages). The treatment regimen is also standard.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(B) *Mycobacterium avium complex* (MAC) infection in severe AIDS:** MAC infection can cause disseminated disease, including GI symptoms (diarrhea, abdominal pain, weight loss) and systemic symptoms (fever, lymphadenopathy). However, it typically occurs in immunocompromised individuals (e.g., AIDS patients), and the characteristic histopathology involves acid-fast bacilli within macrophages, not PAS-positive, diastase-resistant material. The neurological findings described are less typical for MAC.
- **(C) Celiac disease with Marsh type 3 villous atrophy:** Celiac disease causes malabsorption (diarrhea, weight loss, abdominal distension) due to an immune reaction to gluten leading to villous atrophy. While it can cause systemic symptoms, arthralgias, neurological symptoms (ataxia, neuropathy), and hyperpigmentation are less common or present differently. The endoscopic findings might show scalloping, but not typically shaggy plaques. The biopsy shows villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, and increased intraepithelial lymphocytes, not PAS-positive macrophages.
- **(D) Intestinal amyloidosis AL with Apple-green birefringence:** Amyloidosis can affect the GI tract, causing malabsorption, diarrhea, and weight loss. However, the characteristic histopathological finding is the deposition of amyloid protein, which stains with Congo red and exhibits apple-green birefringence under polarized light, not PAS-positive macrophages. Systemic symptoms can occur, but the specific combination presented (arthralgias, neurological signs, plaques) is less typical.
- **(E) Giardiasis with pear-shaped flagellated trophozoites:** Giardiasis is a parasitic infection causing acute or chronic diarrhea, abdominal cramps, and malabsorption. It is diagnosed by identifying *Giardia lamblia* trophozoites or cysts in stool samples or duodenal aspirates/biopsies. The biopsy would show trophozoites attached to the epithelium, not PAS-positive macrophages. While it causes GI symptoms, the systemic features (arthralgias, neurological signs, hyperpigmentation) are not characteristic.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Whipple Disease (A)                                   | MAC Infection (B)                               | Celiac Disease (C)                               | Amyloidosis (D)                                  | Giardiasis (E)                                   |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- |
| **Causative Agent**     | *Tropheryma whipplei* (bacterium)                     | *Mycobacterium avium complex* (bacterium)       | Autoimmune reaction to gluten                   | Amyloid protein deposition                       | *Giardia lamblia* (protozoan)                     |
| **Typical Patient**     | Middle-aged white male                                | Immunocompromised (e.g., AIDS)                  | Any age, genetic predisposition                 | Older adults, associated with plasma cell dyscrasia | Any age, exposure to contaminated water/food      |
| **Key GI Symptoms**     | Diarrhea, malabsorption, weight loss, abdominal pain | Diarrhea, abdominal pain, weight loss           | Diarrhea, steatorrhea, bloating, weight loss     | Diarrhea, malabsorption, weight loss             | Diarrhea, cramps, bloating, malabsorption       |
| **Key Non-GI Symptoms** | Arthralgias, neurological (cognitive, ophthalmoplegia), fever, lymphadenopathy, hyperpigmentation | Fever, weight loss, night sweats, lymphadenopathy | Fatigue, anemia, dermatitis herpetiformis        | Cardiomyopathy, neuropathy, nephrotic syndrome   | None typically, unless severe/prolonged           |
| **Endoscopy**           | Shaggy plaques (duodenum)                             | Variable, may be normal or show inflammation   | Scalloping, mosaic pattern                      | Thickened folds, nodularity                      | Edema, inflammation, sometimes trophozoites seen |
| **Biopsy**              | PAS+, diastase-resistant macrophages in lamina propria | Acid-fast bacilli in macrophages                | Villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, IELs        | Congo red+, apple-green birefringence            | Trophozoites on epithelium                       |
| **Treatment**           | Ceftriaxone + TMP-SMX                                 | Multi-drug anti-TB regimen                      | Gluten-free diet                                 | Chemotherapy (if AL), supportive care            | Metronidazole, tinidazole, nitazoxanide          |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Whipple disease is a systemic infection caused by *Tropheryma whipplei*, characterized by the infiltration of macrophages in the small intestinal lamina propria and other organs. The hallmark histological finding is PAS-positive, diastase-resistant macrophages. Clinical presentation often includes the tetrad of arthralgias, diarrhea/malabsorption, abdominal pain, and weight loss, frequently accompanied by neurological manifestations. Recognition of this specific histopathology is key to diagnosis.

---

### Question 127: Diagnostic Histopathology: Whipple Disease (PAS+ / Acid-Fast -) vs MAC (PAS+ / Acid-Fast +)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating intestinal infections based on histopathological features, specifically PAS staining and acid-fast staining of foamy macrophages in the lamina propria, correlating with specific pathogens (Tropheryma whipplei vs. Mycobacterium avium complex) and patient populations (immunocompromised).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A pathology resident is examining duodenal biopsies from two patients presenting with chronic diarrhea, significant weight loss, and abdominal pain. Both patients are known to be immunocompromised. Microscopic examination of both biopsies reveals infiltration of the lamina propria by numerous foamy macrophages. These macrophages contain abundant cytoplasmic granules that stain positive with Periodic Acid-Schiff (PAS) stain in both specimens. However, when special Ziehl-Neelsen staining is performed, Specimen 1 shows no evidence of acid-fast bacilli within the macrophages, while Specimen 2 demonstrates intensely acid-fast positive bacilli packed within the foamy macrophages. The resident needs to correctly identify the diagnosis associated with each biopsy specimen based on these histopathological findings.

(A) Specimen 1 is Whipple disease (Tropheryma whipplei, PAS-positive, Acid-Fast negative); Specimen 2 is Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC, PAS-positive, Acid-Fast positive, common in advanced HIV with CD4 <50/uL)
(B) Specimen 1 is MAC; Specimen 2 is Whipple disease
(C) Specimen 1 is Celiac disease; Specimen 2 is Crohn disease
(D) Specimen 1 is Giardia lamblia; Specimen 2 is Entamoeba histolytica
(E) Both specimens represent normal small intestinal lymphoid tissue

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Chronic diarrhea, weight loss, abdominal pain in immunocompromised individuals. This suggests an opportunistic infection or malabsorption syndrome.
- **Histopathology:** Duodenal biopsy showing foamy macrophages in the lamina propria. This is a key finding pointing towards certain infections or lipid storage disorders.
- **PAS Staining:** Both specimens show PAS-positive granules within the foamy macrophages. PAS stains glycogen, glycoproteins, glycolipids, and mucopolysaccharides. This finding is characteristic of both Whipple disease and Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC) infection, as the ingested material (Tropheryma whipplei or MAC bacilli) contains PAS-positive components or triggers the accumulation of PAS-positive material within the macrophages.
- **Acid-Fast Staining (Ziehl-Neelsen):** Specimen 1 is acid-fast negative, while Specimen 2 is intensely acid-fast positive. This is the crucial differentiating feature. Acid-fast staining detects organisms with high mycolic acid content in their cell walls, such as Mycobacteria.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The combination of PAS positivity and acid-fast negativity points strongly to Whipple disease, caused by the bacterium *Tropheryma whipplei*. PAS positivity and acid-fast positivity point strongly to infection with Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC).

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (消化道分泌生理与上皮转运机制推导)
- **Whipple Disease:** Caused by *Tropheryma whipplei*, a Gram-positive actinomycete-like bacterium. It primarily affects the small intestine but can disseminate systemically. The bacteria are phagocytosed by macrophages, leading to the characteristic foamy macrophages in the lamina propria. The ingested bacterial material or cellular debris contains PAS-positive components (glycogen-like material), hence the PAS positivity. *T. whipplei* lacks the mycolic acid-rich cell wall characteristic of Mycobacteria, so it does not stain acid-fast. Diagnosis is confirmed by biopsy showing PAS-positive macrophages and negative acid-fast stain, along with PCR or culture for *T. whipplei*. Treatment involves long-term antibiotics.
- **Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC) Infection:** MAC (primarily *M. avium* and *M. intracellulare*) are environmental mycobacteria that can cause opportunistic infections, particularly in immunocompromised individuals, especially those with advanced HIV/AIDS (CD4 count < 50/uL). MAC organisms are phagocytosed by macrophages, leading to foamy macrophages in various organs, including the gastrointestinal tract, liver, spleen, and bone marrow. MAC has a cell wall rich in mycolic acids, making it strongly acid-fast positive. The ingested bacteria or cellular components within the macrophages may also contain PAS-positive material, leading to PAS positivity in addition to acid-fast positivity. Diagnosis involves identifying acid-fast bacilli in tissue biopsies or body fluids, often confirmed by culture.
- **Other Conditions:**
    - **Celiac Disease:** Characterized by villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, and increased intraepithelial lymphocytes, not typically foamy macrophages.
    - **Crohn Disease:** Characterized by transmural inflammation, granulomas (non-caseating), and skip lesions, not typically foamy macrophages.
    - **Giardia lamblia:** A protozoan parasite causing diarrhea. Histology shows trophozoites attached to the epithelium, not foamy macrophages.
    - **Entamoeba histolytica:** A protozoan parasite causing amoebic dysentery. Histology shows trophozoites invading the mucosa, not foamy macrophages.
    - **Normal Lymphoid Tissue:** Contains lymphocytes and plasma cells, not foamy macrophages filled with PAS-positive material.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  Identify the key histopathological findings: Foamy macrophages in the lamina propria of duodenal biopsies.
2.  Note the PAS staining result: Both specimens are PAS-positive. This narrows the differential to conditions where macrophages accumulate PAS-positive material, including Whipple disease and MAC infection.
3.  Note the crucial differentiating feature: Acid-fast staining. Specimen 1 is acid-fast negative, while Specimen 2 is acid-fast positive.
4.  Correlate acid-fast staining with specific pathogens: Acid-fast positivity is characteristic of Mycobacteria, including MAC. Acid-fast negativity rules out MAC and points towards other causes of PAS-positive foamy macrophages.
5.  Identify the pathogen associated with PAS-positive, acid-fast negative foamy macrophages: *Tropheryma whipplei*, the cause of Whipple disease.
6.  Combine the findings: Specimen 1 (PAS+, Acid-Fast-) corresponds to Whipple disease. Specimen 2 (PAS+, Acid-Fast+) corresponds to MAC infection.
7.  Evaluate the options based on this deduction. Option (A) correctly matches Specimen 1 with Whipple disease and Specimen 2 with MAC.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Specimen 1 is Whipple disease (Tropheryma whipplei, PAS-positive, Acid-Fast negative); Specimen 2 is Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC, PAS-positive, Acid-Fast positive, common in advanced HIV with CD4 <50/uL) - CORRECT.** This option accurately reflects the characteristic histopathological findings for both Whipple disease (PAS+, Acid-Fast-) and MAC infection (PAS+, Acid-Fast+). The additional note about MAC being common in advanced HIV is also accurate context.
- **(B) Specimen 1 is MAC; Specimen 2 is Whipple disease - INCORRECT.** This option reverses the correct association based on the acid-fast staining results. MAC is acid-fast positive, while Whipple disease is acid-fast negative.
- **(C) Specimen 1 is Celiac disease; Specimen 2 is Crohn disease - INCORRECT.** Celiac disease and Crohn disease are inflammatory bowel diseases with distinct histological features (villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, intraepithelial lymphocytes for Celiac; transmural inflammation, granulomas for Crohn) but do not typically present with foamy macrophages containing PAS-positive material.
- **(D) Specimen 1 is Giardia lamblia; Specimen 2 is Entamoeba histolytica - INCORRECT.** Giardia and Entamoeba are parasitic infections with characteristic trophozoites or cysts visible on histology, not foamy macrophages.
- **(E) Both specimens represent normal small intestinal lymphoid tissue - INCORRECT.** Normal lymphoid tissue does not contain the characteristic foamy macrophages filled with PAS-positive material seen in these biopsies.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | Whipple Disease (Tropheryma whipplei) | MAC Infection (Mycobacterium avium complex) |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
| Patient Population  | Often immunocompetent, but can occur in HIV | Immunocompromised (esp. advanced HIV)      |
| Histology           | Foamy macrophages in lamina propria    | Foamy macrophages in lamina propria         |
| PAS Stain           | Positive                               | Positive                                   |
| Acid-Fast Stain     | Negative                               | Positive                                   |
| Typical Symptoms    | Diarrhea, weight loss, arthralgia, fever | Diarrhea, weight loss, fever, night sweats |
| Treatment           | Long-term antibiotics                 | Long-term multi-drug anti-mycobacterial therapy |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
This question tests the ability to differentiate between two important causes of foamy macrophage infiltration in the intestinal lamina propria based on specific histochemical stains. Remember that Whipple disease is characterized by PAS-positive, acid-fast negative macrophages, while MAC infection is characterized by PAS-positive, acid-fast positive macrophages. This distinction is crucial for accurate diagnosis and appropriate management in immunocompromised patients.

---

### Question 128: Carbohydrate Malabsorption: Lactase Deficiency, SGLT1 Spared, Hydrogen Breath Test, and Stool Osmotic Gap
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Primary adult-type hypolactasia (lactase deficiency) pathophysiology, including genetic basis, enzyme function, carbohydrate transport (SGLT1, GLUT5), osmotic diarrhea, and bacterial fermentation.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 26-year-old woman of East Asian descent presents with recurrent bloating, audible borborygmi, flatulence, and crampy abdominal pain followed by watery diarrhea that occurs consistently 30 to 90 minutes after consuming dairy products (milk, ice cream). When she eliminates dairy from her diet, her symptoms completely disappear. Diagnostic testing shows a stool pH of 5.0 (acidic) and a high stool osmotic gap (135 mOsm/kg). A hydrogen breath test demonstrates a rise of 32 ppm in breath hydrogen after ingesting 50 g of lactose. Which of the following describes the molecular pathophysiology of Primary Adult-Type Hypolactasia?

(A) Genetically programmed, age-dependent down-regulation of the lactase-phlorizin hydrolase (LCT) gene on the apical brush border of enterocytes (common in >70-90% of adults of East Asian, African, and Mediterranean ancestry); unhydrolyzed lactose cannot be absorbed by SGLT1, remains in the lumen as an osmotic load drawing water, and is fermented by colonic bacteria into short-chain fatty acids, lactic acid (lowering stool pH), and hydrogen gas (detected in breath)
(B) Autoimmune destruction of SGLT1 transporters in the jejunum
(C) Congenital absence of pancreatic amylase
(D) Excessive secretion of hydrochloric acid into the small intestine
(E) Complete absence of the GLUT5 fructose transporter

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 26-year-old woman of East Asian descent. This demographic is highly susceptible to primary adult-type hypolactasia.
- **Symptoms:** Bloating, borborygmi, flatulence, crampy abdominal pain, watery diarrhea occurring 30-90 minutes after dairy consumption. These are classic symptoms of lactose intolerance. The timing suggests a malabsorption issue in the small intestine, not a motility disorder or infection.
- **Dietary Trigger:** Symptoms are specifically linked to dairy products (containing lactose). Symptom resolution upon dairy elimination strongly points to lactose intolerance.
- **Stool pH:** 5.0 (acidic). This indicates bacterial fermentation of unabsorbed carbohydrates (like lactose) in the colon, producing acidic byproducts (lactic acid, short-chain fatty acids).
- **Stool Osmotic Gap:** 135 mOsm/kg. A high stool osmotic gap (>125 mOsm/kg) indicates osmotic diarrhea, where unabsorbed solutes (in this case, lactose) draw water into the intestinal lumen. Calculated as 290 - 2 * ([Na+] + [K+]).
- **Hydrogen Breath Test:** Rise of 32 ppm after lactose ingestion. Unabsorbed lactose reaches the colon, where bacteria ferment it. Hydrogen gas is a byproduct of this fermentation and is absorbed into the bloodstream, eventually exhaled. A significant rise confirms lactose malabsorption.
- **Diagnosis:** The constellation of symptoms, dietary trigger, stool pH, high stool osmotic gap, and positive hydrogen breath test definitively points to lactose intolerance, specifically primary adult-type hypolactasia.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (消化道分泌生理与上皮转运机制推导)
- **Lactose Digestion:** Lactose (galactose + glucose disaccharide) is normally hydrolyzed in the small intestinal brush border by the enzyme lactase (lactase-phlorizin hydrolase, LCT).
- **LCT Gene Regulation:** The LCT gene is normally highly expressed in infants, allowing digestion of lactose in breast milk. In most populations, expression significantly decreases after weaning (primary adult-type hypolactasia), due to epigenetic mechanisms (DNA methylation) and reduced transcription factor activity (e.g., OCT4, SOX9). This is genetically programmed and varies by ethnicity.
- **Lactose Absorption:** The monosaccharides resulting from lactose hydrolysis (glucose and galactose) are absorbed across the apical membrane of enterocytes via the sodium-glucose cotransporter 1 (SGLT1). SGLT1 is a secondary active transporter, utilizing the Na+ gradient maintained by the Na+/K+-ATPase on the basolateral membrane.
- **Pathophysiology of Lactase Deficiency:** In primary adult-type hypolactasia, reduced LCT enzyme activity means lactose is not efficiently hydrolyzed. Unhydrolyzed lactose remains in the intestinal lumen.
- **Osmotic Diarrhea:** Unabsorbed lactose acts as an osmotic agent, drawing water into the lumen, leading to watery diarrhea and increased stool volume. This explains the high stool osmotic gap.
- **Colonic Fermentation:** The unabsorbed lactose passes into the colon, where it is fermented by the gut microbiota. This fermentation produces short-chain fatty acids (SCFAs), lactic acid, carbon dioxide (CO2), and hydrogen gas (H2).
- **Symptoms:** SCFAs and lactic acid lower the colonic pH, contributing to the acidic stool pH (5.0). Gas production (CO2, H2, methane) causes bloating, flatulence, and abdominal cramps.
- **Hydrogen Breath Test:** The hydrogen gas produced by bacterial fermentation is absorbed into the portal circulation and exhaled via the lungs. The hydrogen breath test measures this exhaled H2, indicating colonic fermentation of unabsorbed substrate (lactose).
- **SGLT1 Function:** SGLT1 is responsible for absorbing the *products* of lactose digestion (glucose and galactose), not lactose itself. Lactase deficiency does *not* directly affect SGLT1 function or expression.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Symptoms & Trigger:** Patient has classic symptoms (bloating, diarrhea, cramps, gas) specifically after consuming dairy (lactose). This points to lactose intolerance.
2.  **Demographics:** East Asian descent is a high-risk group for primary adult-type hypolactasia.
3.  **Stool Analysis:** Acidic stool pH (5.0) suggests colonic fermentation of unabsorbed carbohydrates. High stool osmotic gap (135 mOsm/kg) confirms osmotic diarrhea due to unabsorbed solutes.
4.  **Breath Test:** Positive hydrogen breath test confirms bacterial fermentation of lactose in the colon.
5.  **Mechanism:** The underlying cause is the inability to digest lactose due to insufficient lactase enzyme activity. This enzyme is located on the apical brush border of enterocytes and is encoded by the LCT gene, whose expression is developmentally downregulated in most adults. Unhydrolyzed lactose cannot be absorbed by SGLT1, leading to the observed osmotic and fermentation effects.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Genetically programmed, age-dependent down-regulation of the lactase-phlorizin hydrolase (LCT) gene on the apical brush border of enterocytes (common in >70-90% of adults of East Asian, African, and Mediterranean ancestry); unhydrolyzed lactose cannot be absorbed by SGLT1, remains in the lumen as an osmotic load drawing water, and is fermented by colonic bacteria into short-chain fatty acids, lactic acid (lowering stool pH), and hydrogen gas (detected in breath)**: This option accurately describes the pathophysiology of primary adult-type hypolactasia, matching all the clinical findings (demographics, symptoms, stool pH, osmotic gap, breath test). It correctly identifies the enzyme (lactase/LCT), its location (apical brush border), the genetic basis (age-dependent downregulation), the consequence of deficiency (unabsorbed lactose), the mechanisms of diarrhea (osmotic load) and other symptoms (fermentation products, gas), and the role of SGLT1 (absorbing monosaccharides, not lactose). This is the correct answer.
- **(B) Autoimmune destruction of SGLT1 transporters in the jejunum**: This describes a rare condition causing glucose/galactose malabsorption, not lactose intolerance. Symptoms would include diarrhea after consuming glucose/galactose, not specifically lactose. Lactase enzyme activity would be normal. SGLT1 is not the primary target in lactose intolerance.
- **(C) Congenital absence of pancreatic amylase**: This would cause carbohydrate malabsorption (starch, glycogen) but not specifically lactose intolerance. Symptoms would occur after consuming starchy foods. Lactose is digested by lactase in the small intestine, not amylase from the pancreas.
- **(D) Excessive secretion of hydrochloric acid into the small intestine**: This is not a physiological process. Excessive gastric acid secretion (e.g., Zollinger-Ellison syndrome) causes peptic ulcers and potentially diarrhea due to acid inactivation of pancreatic enzymes or damage to the intestinal mucosa, but not specifically lactose intolerance symptoms or the characteristic stool findings (acidic pH, high osmotic gap, positive breath test).
- **(E) Complete absence of the GLUT5 fructose transporter**: This would cause fructose malabsorption. Symptoms would occur after consuming fructose-containing foods (fruits, honey, high-fructose corn syrup). GLUT5 transports fructose across the apical membrane; its absence doesn't affect lactose digestion or absorption.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | Lactase Deficiency (Primary Adult-Type) | SGLT1 Deficiency (Rare) | Pancreatic Amylase Deficiency (Congenital) | Excessive Gastric Acid (Pathological) | GLUT5 Deficiency (Fructose Malabsorption) |
| :------------------ | :-------------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**  | ↓ Lactase (LCT gene downregulation)     | ↓ SGLT1                 | Absent Pancreatic Amylase                 | N/A (Pathological)                    | ↓ GLUT5                                   |
| **Affected Sugar**  | Lactose                                 | Glucose, Galactose      | Starch, Glycogen                            | N/A                                    | Fructose                                   |
| **Trigger Foods**   | Dairy Products                          | Sugary foods/drinks     | Starchy foods                               | N/A                                    | Fruits, Honey, HFCS                       |
| **Mechanism**       | Unhydrolyzed lactose -> Osmotic diarrhea, Fermentation | Impaired glucose/galactose absorption -> Osmotic diarrhea | Impaired starch digestion -> Osmotic diarrhea | Acid damage/enzyme inactivation -> Diarrhea | Impaired fructose absorption -> Osmotic diarrhea |
| **Stool Osmotic Gap** | High                                    | High                   | High                                       | Variable                               | High                                       |
| **Stool pH**        | Acidic (fermentation)                   | Variable               | Variable                                   | Variable                               | Acidic (fermentation)                   |
| **Breath Test**     | ↑ H2 after Lactose                     | N/A                    | N/A                                        | N/A                                    | ↑ H2 after Fructose                     |
| **SGLT1 Role**      | Normal (absorbs glucose/galactose)      | Defective              | Normal                                     | Normal                                 | Normal                                     |
| **Lactase Role**    | Defective                               | Normal                 | Normal                                     | Normal                                 | Normal                                     |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Primary adult-type hypolactasia is a common genetically determined condition resulting from the age-dependent downregulation of the LCT gene, leading to lactase deficiency. This prevents lactose hydrolysis, causing unabsorbed lactose to act as an osmotic load in the small intestine and substrate for colonic bacterial fermentation, resulting in osmotic diarrhea, gas production, bloating, and acidic stools. The hydrogen breath test is a key diagnostic tool. SGLT1, responsible for absorbing the monosaccharides glucose and galactose, remains functional.

---

### Question 129: Carbohydrate Malabsorption: Secondary Lactase Deficiency, Villous Tip Sloughing, and Recovery
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Transient secondary lactase deficiency following viral gastroenteritis due to selective damage to mature villous tip enterocytes expressing lactase, leading to carbohydrate malabsorption. (病毒性肠胃炎后短暂性继发性乳糖不耐受，因表达乳糖酶的成熟绒毛尖端肠细胞选择性损伤，导致碳水化合物吸收不良。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-year-old boy presents with a history of acute rotavirus gastroenteritis 4 days prior, characterized by vomiting and fever. His symptoms have resolved, and his appetite has returned. However, upon reintroduction of whole cow's milk, he experiences immediate explosive, watery, acidic diarrhea accompanied by perianal erythema and skin excoriation. These symptoms completely resolve when his diet is switched to a lactose-free soy-based formula. The question asks for the physiological mechanism underlying this transient lactose intolerance following viral gastroenteritis.

(A) Lactase is an integral membrane glycoprotein located exclusively at the very tips (apices) of the intestinal villi (the most mature and vulnerable enterocytes); viral infection (rotavirus/norovirus) selectively infects, blunts, and sloughs the villous tips, causing transient lactase deficiency until the crypt stem cells proliferate and regenerate mature villous tips over 2 to 4 weeks.
(B) Rotavirus permanently mutates the child's LCT gene.
(C) The child developed an immediate IgE-mediated milk allergy.
(D) Rotavirus produces a specific antibody that destroys all dietary lactose.
(E) Viral toxins permanently paralyze the colon.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 2-year-old boy.
- **History:** Recent (4 days ago) acute rotavirus gastroenteritis (vomiting, fever).
- **Current Symptoms:** Explosive, watery, acidic diarrhea, perianal erythema/excoriation upon consuming whole cow's milk.
- **Resolution:** Symptoms resolve completely upon switching to lactose-free formula.
- **Key Clue:** Transient lactose intolerance *following* viral gastroenteritis, specifically triggered by lactose (in milk). The symptoms (watery diarrhea, acidic stool, perianal irritation) are classic for osmotic diarrhea due to unabsorbed lactose fermentation by colonic bacteria producing short-chain fatty acids (SCFAs) and gases (H2, CO2, CH4), lowering luminal pH. The transient nature and specific trigger (lactose) point towards a temporary deficiency in lactase activity, not a permanent allergy or global malabsorption.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (消化道分泌生理与上皮转运机制推导)
- **Lactase (LCT):** A brush-border enzyme (beta-galactosidase) located on the apical membrane of mature enterocytes, specifically at the tips of the intestinal villi. It hydrolyzes lactose (a disaccharide) into glucose and galactose (monosaccharides), which are then absorbed via SGLT1 (Na+-glucose cotransporter) and GLUT2/GLUT5 transporters, respectively.
- **Intestinal Epithelial Cell Turnover:** The intestinal epithelium has a rapid turnover rate (every 3-5 days). Enterocytes originate from crypt stem cells, migrate up the crypt-villus axis, differentiate, and mature at the villous tips, where they express specific brush-border enzymes like lactase. Mature enterocytes are eventually shed from the villous tips.
- **Viral Gastroenteritis (e.g., Rotavirus):** These viruses primarily infect and damage the mature enterocytes located at the tips of the villi. This leads to villous blunting (shortening) and sloughing (shedding) of the mature cells, including those expressing lactase.
- **Secondary Lactase Deficiency:** The damage to the villous tips results in a temporary reduction in the surface area available for lactose digestion and a decrease in the amount of functional lactase enzyme. This is termed "secondary lactase deficiency" because the underlying genetic capacity to produce lactase (LCT gene) is intact.
- **Recovery:** As the viral infection resolves, the crypt stem cells proliferate and differentiate, gradually regenerating the villous epithelium and restoring the population of mature enterocytes expressing lactase. This process typically takes 2-4 weeks, explaining the transient nature of the lactose intolerance.
- **Other Disaccharidases:** While viral gastroenteritis can affect the entire epithelium, the selective damage to the mature villous tips disproportionately affects lactase, which is highly concentrated at this location. Other disaccharidases (e.g., sucrase, maltase) are also located at the villous tips but may be less vulnerable or the overall epithelial damage might not be severe enough to cause significant deficiency of these enzymes, or their substrates are less osmotically active.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  The patient has a history of recent viral gastroenteritis (rotavirus).
2.  Following the infection, he develops symptoms specifically after consuming milk (containing lactose).
3.  The symptoms (watery, acidic diarrhea, perianal irritation) are characteristic of lactose malabsorption.
4.  The symptoms resolve when lactose is removed from the diet (lactose-free formula).
5.  This indicates a temporary inability to digest lactose.
6.  Viral gastroenteritis is known to cause transient damage to the intestinal epithelium, particularly the mature villous tips.
7.  Lactase is primarily located at the villous tips.
8.  Therefore, damage to the villous tips by rotavirus leads to a temporary deficiency of lactase, causing secondary lactose intolerance.
9.  The recovery of the epithelium over weeks explains the transient nature of the intolerance.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Lactase is an integral membrane glycoprotein located exclusively at the very tips (apices) of the intestinal villi (the most mature and vulnerable enterocytes); viral infection (rotavirus/norovirus) selectively infects, blunts, and sloughs the villous tips, causing transient lactase deficiency until the crypt stem cells proliferate and regenerate mature villous tips over 2 to 4 weeks.** This option accurately describes the location of lactase, the effect of viral gastroenteritis on the villous tips, the mechanism of secondary lactase deficiency, and the transient nature of the condition due to epithelial regeneration. This is the correct explanation.
- **(B) Rotavirus permanently mutates the child's LCT gene.** This is incorrect. Rotavirus is a viral infection, not a mutagen targeting the LCT gene. Primary lactase deficiency (lactase non-persistence) is due to genetic mutations affecting LCT gene expression, typically occurring after infancy, not caused by acute viral infection.
- **(C) The child developed an immediate IgE-mediated milk allergy.** This is incorrect. Milk allergy involves an immune response (IgE antibodies) to milk proteins (e.g., casein, whey). Symptoms can include hives, vomiting, diarrhea, anaphylaxis, but the diarrhea is not specifically triggered by lactose itself, and the mechanism is immunological, not enzymatic deficiency. The resolution with a lactose-free formula specifically points to carbohydrate malabsorption, not protein allergy.
- **(D) Rotavirus produces a specific antibody that destroys all dietary lactose.** This is incorrect. Viruses do not produce antibodies. Antibodies are produced by the host immune system. Furthermore, lactose is a sugar molecule, not a protein or cell, and antibodies do not directly "destroy" dietary lactose. The problem is the lack of the enzyme (lactase) to break it down.
- **(E) Viral toxins permanently paralyze the colon.** This is incorrect. While some bacterial toxins (e.g., from Clostridium difficile) can cause colonic paralysis (toxic megacolon), viral gastroenteritis primarily affects the small intestine (enterocytes), causing malabsorption and diarrhea due to increased secretion and reduced absorption, not colonic paralysis. The symptoms described (watery diarrhea, perianal irritation) are consistent with small bowel carbohydrate malabsorption, not colonic dysmotility.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Primary Lactase Deficiency (Lactase Non-Persistence) | Secondary Lactase Deficiency (Post-Gastroenteritis) | Milk Allergy (IgE-Mediated) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------- |
| **Cause**               | Genetic mutation (LCT gene regulation)               | Damage to mature villous tip enterocytes          | Immune response to milk proteins |
| **Onset**               | Typically after weaning (infancy/childhood)           | Following acute gastroenteritis                    | Variable, often early childhood |
| **Duration**            | Permanent                                            | Transient (weeks)                                 | Can be lifelong             |
| **Affected Enzyme**     | Lactase                                              | Lactase (primarily)                               | N/A                         |
| **Mechanism**           | Reduced lactase gene expression                      | Loss of lactase-expressing cells                  | IgE-mediated inflammation   |
| **Symptoms Trigger**    | Lactose ingestion                                    | Lactose ingestion                                    | Milk protein ingestion      |
| **Typical Symptoms**    | Diarrhea, bloating, gas after lactose                 | Diarrhea, bloating, gas after lactose                 | Hives, vomiting, diarrhea, anaphylaxis |
| **Resolution**          | Avoidance of lactose                                 | Resolution with epithelial regeneration            | Avoidance of milk proteins  |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Acute viral gastroenteritis, particularly rotavirus, commonly causes transient secondary lactose intolerance. This occurs because the virus damages the mature enterocytes located at the tips of the intestinal villi, where lactase enzyme is highly concentrated. The resulting loss of lactase activity leads to malabsorption of lactose, causing osmotic diarrhea. The condition is transient as the intestinal epithelium regenerates over several weeks. This mechanism highlights the vulnerability of mature villous tip cells and the importance of lactase location in the context of intestinal injury.

---

### Question 130: Carbohydrate Malabsorption: Congenital Sucrase-Isomaltase Deficiency, Weaning, and Sacrosidase
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of carbohydrate digestion and absorption, specifically focusing on the role of disaccharidases (sucrase, isomaltase) located on the brush border of the small intestinal enterocytes, the consequences of their deficiency, and the appropriate diagnostic and therapeutic approaches. It also requires differentiating this condition from other causes of malabsorption and diarrhea in infants.

#### Clinical Vignette:
An 8-month-old infant, previously thriving on exclusive breastfeeding, is introduced to solid foods, including fruit purees (containing sucrose and starch) and sweetened juices. Within days of this dietary change, the infant develops severe abdominal distension, failure to thrive (weight gain ceases), and explosive watery diarrhea. Stool analysis reveals an acidic pH (<5.0) and the presence of high levels of reducing substances. A duodenal biopsy shows normal villous architecture, indicating no significant structural damage. However, a disaccharidase assay performed on the biopsy specimen reveals absent sucrase activity and a marked reduction in isomaltase activity. What is the most likely diagnosis, and what is the appropriate oral enzyme replacement therapy?

(A) Congenital Sucrase-Isomaltase Deficiency (CSID), an autosomal recessive defect in the SI gene; treated with dietary sucrose/starch restriction and oral Sacrosidase (exogenous yeast-derived sucrase liquid enzyme taken with meals)
(B) Celiac disease; treated with a gluten-free diet
(C) Lactose intolerance; treated with lactase drops
(D) Cystic fibrosis; treated with pancrelipase
(E) Glucose-galactose malabsorption; treated with insulin

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Trigger:** An 8-month-old infant develops symptoms *after* introduction of solid foods containing sucrose (from fruits/juices) and starch (from grains/cereals). This timing strongly suggests a problem with the digestion of these specific carbohydrates. Breast milk contains lactose, but minimal sucrose or complex starches.
- **Symptoms:** Severe abdominal distension, failure to thrive, and explosive watery diarrhea are classic signs of carbohydrate malabsorption. Undigested carbohydrates in the intestinal lumen increase osmotic load, drawing water in (osmotic diarrhea) and leading to gas production by colonic bacteria (distension, flatulence). Failure to thrive results from malabsorption of calories and nutrients.
- **Stool Characteristics:** Acidic stool pH (<5.0) indicates the presence of undigested sugars (like sucrose, glucose, fructose) that are fermented by colonic bacteria into short-chain fatty acids (SCFAs), primarily lactic acid and acetic acid, lowering the pH. High stool reducing substances (detected by tests like Clinitest) confirm the presence of unabsorbed monosaccharides and disaccharides (sucrose, lactose, maltose, glucose, fructose, galactose) that reduce copper sulfate in the test reagent.
- **Duodenal Biopsy:** Normal villous architecture rules out conditions causing significant structural damage to the small intestine, such as celiac disease or severe infectious enteritis. This points towards a functional defect rather than a structural one.
- **Disaccharidase Assay:** Absent sucrase and markedly reduced isomaltase activity directly implicate a deficiency in the enzymes responsible for breaking down sucrose (glucose + fructose) and isomaltose/alpha-dextrins (breakdown products of starch).

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (消化道分泌生理与上皮转运机制推导)
- **Carbohydrate Digestion:** Dietary carbohydrates (sucrose, starch, lactose) are too large to be absorbed by enterocytes.
    - **Sucrose:** Digested by sucrase (a complex of beta-fructofuranosidase and invertase) located on the brush border of enterocytes into glucose and fructose.
    - **Starch:** Digested by salivary amylase (mouth) and pancreatic amylase (duodenum) into smaller oligosaccharides (dextrins, maltose, isomaltose). Final digestion of these oligosaccharides, especially isomaltose and alpha-dextrins, requires brush border enzymes like isomaltase (alpha-dextrinase) and maltase.
    - **Lactose:** Digested by lactase (beta-galactosidase) on the brush border into glucose and galactose.
- **Brush Border Enzymes:** These enzymes are integral membrane proteins located on the microvilli (brush border) of mature enterocytes. They are synthesized in the endoplasmic reticulum and Golgi apparatus and targeted to the apical membrane.
- **Congenital Sucrase-Isomaltase Deficiency (CSID):** An autosomal recessive genetic disorder caused by mutations in the *SI* gene, which encodes the sucrase-isomaltase complex. This leads to a deficiency or absence of both sucrase and isomaltase activity.
- **Pathophysiology of CSID:** Ingestion of sucrose or starch leads to undigested carbohydrates reaching the colon. Bacterial fermentation produces SCFAs, hydrogen gas, and carbon dioxide, causing osmotic diarrhea, abdominal distension, pain, and flatulence. The acidic stool pH and high reducing substances are direct consequences of this fermentation.
- **Diagnosis:** Clinical presentation triggered by sucrose/starch introduction, acidic stool pH, positive reducing substances, normal duodenal biopsy, and specific disaccharidase assay confirming sucrase/isomaltase deficiency.
- **Treatment:** Strict dietary restriction of sucrose and starch is the cornerstone. Oral enzyme replacement therapy with exogenous yeast-derived sucrase (Sacrosidase) taken with meals containing sucrose or starch can improve digestion and reduce symptoms.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Patient Presentation:** Infant develops symptoms (diarrhea, distension, FTT) specifically after introducing sucrose/starch-containing foods. This points to a defect in digesting these carbohydrates.
2.  **Stool Analysis:** Acidic pH and high reducing substances confirm the presence of unabsorbed sugars undergoing bacterial fermentation in the colon.
3.  **Biopsy:** Normal villi rule out structural damage (e.g., celiac disease).
4.  **Enzyme Assay:** Absent sucrase and low isomaltase confirm the specific enzymatic defect.
5.  **Conclusion:** The constellation of findings (timing, symptoms, stool tests, biopsy, enzyme assay) is diagnostic for Congenital Sucrase-Isomaltase Deficiency (CSID).
6.  **Treatment:** The standard treatment involves dietary restriction of sucrose/starch and oral enzyme replacement (Sacrosidase).

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(B) Celiac disease:** Presents with malabsorption, diarrhea, FTT, often triggered by gluten introduction. However, duodenal biopsy typically shows villous atrophy, crypt hyperplasia, and increased intraepithelial lymphocytes, which is absent here. Stool tests are non-specific. Treatment is a gluten-free diet, not Sacrosidase.
- **(C) Lactose intolerance:** Caused by lactase deficiency. Symptoms occur after lactose ingestion (milk, dairy). Stool pH is usually neutral or slightly acidic, not strongly acidic (<5.0). Reducing substances may be present but are primarily due to lactose. Treatment is lactase drops or lactose restriction, not Sacrosidase. The trigger here is sucrose/starch, not lactose.
- **(D) Cystic fibrosis:** A multisystem genetic disorder affecting exocrine glands. Pancreatic insufficiency leads to malabsorption, particularly of fats (steatorrhea), but also carbohydrates and proteins. Symptoms often present earlier, even with breast milk. Stool is typically bulky, greasy, and foul-smelling. Diagnosis involves sweat chloride test or CFTR gene mutation analysis. Treatment involves pancreatic enzyme replacement (pancrelipase), not Sacrosidase. The specific enzyme deficiency here is sucrase/isomaltase, not pancreatic enzymes.
- **(E) Glucose-galactose malabsorption:** A rare autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in the *SLC5A1* gene, encoding the sodium-glucose cotransporter 1 (SGLT1) in the small intestine. This impairs the absorption of glucose and galactose. Symptoms (diarrhea, dehydration) typically start early, even with breast milk (lactose contains galactose). Stool pH is usually acidic due to bacterial fermentation of unabsorbed monosaccharides. Treatment involves avoiding sucrose, glucose, and galactose, not insulin. Insulin is used for diabetes mellitus, not carbohydrate malabsorption. The primary defect is intestinal absorption, not enzymatic digestion of sucrose/starch.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Condition                     | Trigger              | Key Symptoms                               | Stool pH | Reducing Substances | Biopsy Findings      | Enzyme Defect/Deficiency | Treatment                                     |
| :---------------------------- | :------------------- | :----------------------------------------- | :------- | :------------------ | :------------------- | :----------------------- | :-------------------------------------------- |
| CSID                          | Sucrose/Starch       | Diarrhea, distension, FTT                  | <5.0     | High               | Normal               | Sucrase, Isomaltase      | Sucrose/Starch restriction, Sacrosidase       |
| Celiac Disease                | Gluten               | Diarrhea, FTT, abdominal pain               | Variable | Variable           | Villous atrophy      | N/A (Immune mediated)   | Gluten-free diet                             |
| Lactose Intolerance           | Lactose              | Diarrhea, bloating, gas                     | Neutral/Slightly Acidic | Variable           | Normal               | Lactase                 | Lactase drops, Lactose restriction            |
| Cystic Fibrosis (Pancreatic Insufficiency) | Fat, Protein, Carbs | Steatorrhea, FTT, greasy stools            | Variable | Variable           | Normal               | Pancreatic Enzymes      | Pancrelipase                                  |
| Glucose-Galactose Malabsorption | Glucose/Galactose    | Diarrhea, dehydration (often early onset) | <5.0     | High               | Normal               | SGLT1 (absorption)      | Avoid Glucose/Galactose/Sucrose               |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
This case highlights the importance of considering specific enzyme deficiencies when evaluating carbohydrate malabsorption in infants. The clinical presentation, triggered by the introduction of specific carbohydrates (sucrose and starch), combined with acidic stool pH, high reducing substances, normal intestinal biopsy, and specific disaccharidase assay results, points definitively to Congenital Sucrase-Isomaltase Deficiency (CSID). The appropriate treatment involves dietary restriction of the offending carbohydrates and oral enzyme replacement therapy (Sacrosidase). This differentiates CSID from other common causes of infant diarrhea and malabsorption like celiac disease, lactose intolerance, cystic fibrosis, and glucose-galactose malabsorption, each having distinct triggers, pathophysiology, diagnostic findings, and treatments.

---

### Question 131: Carbohydrate Transport: Congenital SGLT1 Mutation, Watery Diarrhea on Glucose, and Fructose Tolerance
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of apical carbohydrate transport mechanisms in the small intestine, specifically the roles of SGLT1 (Na+/glucose/galactose cotransporter) and GLUT5 (fructose transporter), and their relation to osmotic diarrhea. It also requires differentiating between various causes of carbohydrate malabsorption.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-week-old neonate presents with severe, life-threatening watery osmotic diarrhea and hypernatremic dehydration immediately after initiating feeding with breast milk, standard cow's milk formula (containing lactose), or electrolyte solutions containing glucose. The infant's serum sodium is 155 mEq/L (normal 135-145 mEq/L), serum potassium is 6.0 mEq/L (normal 3.5-5.0 mEq/L), and serum chloride is 100 mEq/L (normal 98-107 mEq/L). Stool studies show a high stool osmotic gap (calculated as 290 - 2 * (stool Na+ + stool K+)) > 100 mOsm/kg, indicating an osmotic diarrhea. When the infant is switched to an experimental formula where the sole carbohydrate is Fructose, the diarrhea immediately and completely ceases, and the infant begins gaining weight normally. Which of the following membrane transport defects explains why the infant thrives on fructose but develops massive diarrhea on glucose and galactose?

(A) Congenital Glucose-Galactose Malabsorption caused by an autosomal recessive loss-of-function mutation in the SLC5A1 gene encoding the apical SGLT1 (Na+/glucose/galactose) cotransporter; apical transport of Fructose via the facilitated diffusion transporter GLUT5 is completely preserved and unaffected
(B) Defect in the basolateral GLUT2 transporter affecting all carbohydrates
(C) Defect in the apical GLUT5 transporter
(D) Complete absence of the brush border enzyme maltase
(E) Loss of the cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator CFTR

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 2-week-old neonate with severe watery osmotic diarrhea and hypernatremic dehydration triggered by glucose, galactose (from lactose), and sucrose (from breast milk/formula).
- **Key Finding:** Diarrhea resolves completely when the sole carbohydrate is fructose.
- **Lab Findings:** Hypernatremia (155 mEq/L) and hyperkalemia (6.0 mEq/L) suggest significant fluid and electrolyte loss. High stool osmotic gap (>100 mOsm/kg) confirms osmotic diarrhea, meaning the stool osmolality is much higher than expected based on stool electrolytes alone, indicating the presence of poorly absorbed solutes drawing water into the lumen.
- **Interpretation:** The infant cannot absorb glucose and galactose effectively, leading to their accumulation in the intestinal lumen. These non-absorbed carbohydrates act as osmotic agents, drawing water into the lumen and causing massive watery diarrhea. The infant tolerates fructose, implying fructose absorption is intact. This points to a specific defect in the absorption of glucose and galactose, but not fructose.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (消化道分泌生理与上皮转运机制推导)
- **Apical Carbohydrate Transport:**
    - **Glucose and Galactose:** Absorbed in the small intestine via the apical Sodium-Glucose Linked Transporter 1 (SGLT1), encoded by the *SLC5A1* gene. This is a secondary active transport mechanism, coupling the downhill movement of Na+ (via the Na+/K+-ATPase on the basolateral membrane) to the uphill transport of glucose and galactose against their concentration gradients.
    - **Fructose:** Absorbed in the small intestine via the apical facilitated diffusion transporter GLUT5, encoded by the *SLC2A5* gene. This is a passive process, driven by the concentration gradient of fructose.
- **Basolateral Transport:** After entering the enterocyte, glucose, galactose, and fructose are transported out of the cell into the bloodstream via the basolateral transporter GLUT2, encoded by the *SLC2A2* gene.
- **Pathophysiology:** The clinical scenario describes a selective inability to absorb glucose and galactose, while fructose absorption is normal. This pattern is characteristic of a defect in the SGLT1 transporter, which is responsible for the uptake of glucose and galactose. A defect in GLUT5 would impair fructose absorption. A defect in GLUT2 would impair the exit of all three monosaccharides (glucose, galactose, fructose) from the enterocyte, leading to diarrhea triggered by all three. A defect in maltase would impair the breakdown of maltose into glucose, but glucose itself would still be absorbed via SGLT1. A defect in CFTR primarily affects chloride and bicarbonate secretion, leading to secretory diarrhea, not osmotic diarrhea specifically triggered by glucose/galactose.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  The infant develops severe osmotic diarrhea upon ingestion of glucose and galactose (present in lactose, sucrose, and glucose solutions).
2.  The diarrhea resolves completely when fructose is the sole carbohydrate source.
3.  This selective pattern strongly suggests a defect specifically affecting the absorption of glucose and galactose, but not fructose.
4.  The primary apical transporters for glucose and galactose are SGLT1, while the primary transporter for fructose is GLUT5.
5.  Therefore, the defect must lie in the SGLT1 transporter or its associated mechanism.
6.  Option A describes a congenital defect in SGLT1 (SLC5A1 gene), which perfectly explains the observed clinical picture: impaired glucose/galactose absorption leading to osmotic diarrhea, while fructose absorption via GLUT5 remains intact.
7.  Option B (GLUT2 defect) would cause diarrhea with all three sugars.
8.  Option C (GLUT5 defect) would cause diarrhea with fructose.
9.  Option D (maltase defect) would cause diarrhea primarily after maltose ingestion, but glucose absorption itself would be intact via SGLT1.
10. Option E (CFTR defect) causes secretory diarrhea, not osmotic diarrhea specifically linked to glucose/galactose malabsorption.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Congenital Glucose-Galactose Malabsorption caused by an autosomal recessive loss-of-function mutation in the SLC5A1 gene encoding the apical SGLT1 (Na+/glucose/galactose) cotransporter; apical transport of Fructose via the facilitated diffusion transporter GLUT5 is completely preserved and unaffected:** This option correctly identifies the specific transporter (SGLT1) responsible for glucose and galactose uptake and explains why fructose absorption (via GLUT5) is unaffected, leading to the observed clinical picture. This is the correct answer.
- **(B) Defect in the basolateral GLUT2 transporter affecting all carbohydrates:** GLUT2 is responsible for the exit of glucose, galactose, and fructose from the enterocyte into the bloodstream. A defect here would impair the absorption of *all three* monosaccharides, leading to diarrhea triggered by glucose, galactose, *and* fructose. This contradicts the clinical finding that the infant tolerates fructose.
- **(C) Defect in the apical GLUT5 transporter:** GLUT5 is the primary transporter for fructose absorption. A defect in GLUT5 would impair fructose absorption, leading to diarrhea when fructose is ingested. This contradicts the clinical finding that the infant thrives on fructose.
- **(D) Complete absence of the brush border enzyme maltase:** Maltase breaks down maltose (a disaccharide) into two glucose molecules. While a lack of maltase would cause diarrhea after consuming maltose-containing foods, the absorption of glucose itself (from other sources like lactose breakdown or direct ingestion) would still occur via SGLT1. This does not explain the specific intolerance to glucose and galactose seen in the vignette.
- **(E) Loss of the cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator CFTR:** CFTR is a chloride channel involved in regulating fluid and electrolyte transport. Its dysfunction leads to impaired chloride and bicarbonate secretion into the intestinal lumen, resulting in decreased water secretion and potentially secretory diarrhea (though typically associated with cystic fibrosis, which presents differently). It does not directly affect carbohydrate absorption and would not cause osmotic diarrhea specifically triggered by glucose and galactose.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Transporter | Location | Substrates | Mechanism | Clinical Relevance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SGLT1 | Apical | Glucose, Galactose | Secondary Active (Na+ cotransport) | Glucose-Galactose Malabsorption (SLC5A1 mutation) |
| GLUT5 | Apical | Fructose | Facilitated Diffusion | Essential Fructose Malabsorption (SLC2A5 mutation) |
| GLUT2 | Basolateral | Glucose, Galactose, Fructose | Facilitated Diffusion | Impairs exit of all monosaccharides from enterocyte |
| Maltase | Brush Border | Maltose | Enzymatic Hydrolysis | Maltose Intolerance |
| CFTR | Apical (Crypts) | Chloride, Bicarbonate | Channel | Cystic Fibrosis, Secretory Diarrhea |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
This case highlights the distinct roles of apical intestinal transporters SGLT1 (for glucose/galactose) and GLUT5 (for fructose) in carbohydrate absorption. Understanding these specific transport mechanisms is crucial for diagnosing conditions like congenital glucose-galactose malabsorption (SGLT1 defect) or essential fructosuria/malabsorption (GLUT5 defect), which present with characteristic patterns of carbohydrate intolerance and osmotic diarrhea. The resolution of diarrhea with fructose intake specifically points to an intact GLUT5 pathway and a defective SGLT1 pathway.

---

### Question 132: Lipid Malabsorption: Abetalipoproteinemia, MTTP Mutation, Absent ApoB-48/100, and Acanthocytosis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of lipid digestion, absorption, lipoprotein assembly, and the pathophysiology of Abetalipoproteinemia, specifically the role of the MTTP gene and its impact on chylomicron and VLDL formation, leading to fat malabsorption and neurological symptoms due to vitamin E deficiency.

#### Clinical Vignette:
